## Document 065: 2026年9月经济数据点评：总量企稳的读数，结构分化的底色

[Table_ReportInfo] 2026 年 10 月 19 日      
证 券研究报告 •宏观简评报告      
数据点评  
总量企稳的读数，结构分化的底色  
——2026 年 9 月经济数据点评

点评

西 南证券研究 院 
[Table_Summary]  摘要：GDP方面，2026年第三季度我国 GDP 同比增长 4.5%，较二季度回升
0.2 个百分点；前三季度增长 4.6%，仍处于全年 4.5%-5%的目标区间内。出
口和高技术制造业是主要支撑，全球 AI 产业景气带动电子产业链中游需求保
持强劲，但投资、消费恢复偏慢。预计四季度GDP 同比增长 4.5%-4.7%，出
口韧性与政策支持有望托底， 但地产调整和消费偏弱仍将制约经济增长。居民
收支和就业方面，前三季度居民人均可支配收入名义增长5.3%，增速较上半
年回升；人均消费支出名义增长 3.6%，消费增速未随收入同步加快。前三季
度城镇调查失业率平均为5.21%， 稳就业、促增收和职业技能培训政策有望改
善居民收入预期， 但消费倾向回升仍需观察。工业生产方面，前三季度规上工
业增加值同比增长 5.3%，9 月单月增长 5.5%，较 8 月提高 0.3 个百分点。高
技术产业和装备制造业增加值分别增长14.3%和 10.2%，AI 电子链、专用设
备及船舶航空等行业表现较强，但部分高景气产品增速放缓。预计全年规上工
业增加值增速为 5.2%-5.4%， 高技术制造业、 装备制造业及出口仍是主要支撑。
固定资产投资方面，前三季度固定资产投资同比下降 7.2%，扣除房地产开发
投资后下降 4.2%。高技术产业投资增长 4.9%。基建投资同比下降 4.2%，但
信息传输、 航空运输和水上运输业投资保持较快增长。剩余专项债、地方债结
存限额及新型政策性金融工具有望支撑基建和制造业投资。房地产投资方面，
前三季度房地产开发投资同比下降20.6%，9 月单月下降 27.19%，降幅继续
扩大。新开工、 竣工面积分别下降24.9%和 25.1%，商品房销售面积、销售额
分别下降 13.2%和 13.9%， 房企到位资金下降21.6%， 行业供需和资金面仍然
承压。9 月房价环比表现有所改善，购房贷款贴息政策也有望降低居民购房成
本。预计四季度销售降幅可能在贴息政策和低基数带动下收窄，但趋势性回暖
仍需观察。制造业投资方面， 前三季度制造业投资同比下降2.1%， 降幅较1-8
月收窄 0.2 个百分点；9 月单月下降 0.95%，降幅明显收窄，但部分改善可能
来自前期需求集中释放和低基数。铁路、船舶、航空航天及其他运输设备制造
业投资增长 15.8%，计算机、通信和其他电子设备制造业增长9.6%。设备工
器具购置增速由一季度的 13.9%回落至前三季度的 9.7%，显示“两新”政策
拉动有所减弱。 预计四季度制造业投资单月增速在零附近波动，累计降幅有望
收窄至 1.5%-2.0%，行业分化或将延续。消费方面，前三季度社零同比增长
1.1%， 商品和服务零售总额增长2.6%， 其中服务零售额增长5.0%， 快于商品
零售的 1.0%。9 月单月社零增长 1.1%，较 8 月回升 0.7 个百分点，中秋错期
和低基数对增速形成提振。通讯器材类和烟酒类零售额分别增长 31.1%和
13.8%，而家电类增长 1.9%、汽车类下降 21.2%，汽车消费仍是主要拖累。
去年同期集中购车推高基数，今年补贴力度收缩、 部分需求提前释放，进一步
制约汽车消费。预计全年社零增速中枢约 1.4%；四季度低基数有望带动增速
小幅回升，但消费整体仍处于低位企稳阶段，趋势性复苏动力不足。 
 总量落在目标区间，出口走强与内需拖累并存。按不变价计算，2026 年第三
季度我国 GDP同比增长 4.5%，较二季度回升 0.2 个百分点，高于 Wind 一致
预期的 4.41%。2026 年前三季度 GDP 同比增长 4.6%，较上半年回落 0.1 个
百分点， 但仍高于Wind一致预期的 4.56%， 增速仍处于全年4.5%-5%的预期
目标区间内。 分产业看， 第三季度第一、 二、 三产业同比分别增长3.6%、3.4%

和 5.2%，其中第一产业增速回落 0.1 个百分点，二、三产业增速均回升。从
增长动力看，出口和高技术制造业是三季度经济增长的重要支撑。 受益于全球
AI 产业景气， 电子产业链中游产品需求保持强劲， 前三季度进出口表现超出市
场预期， 高技术产品出口的竞争优势持续巩固。与出口和制造业生产的较强韧
性相比，内需表现仍相对偏弱，投资和消费恢复偏慢，经济增长的内生动力有
待增强。三季度名义GDP 增速约为 6%，GDP 平减指数连续两个季度为正，
三季度初步核算为 1.33%，价格因素对名义经济增长的支撑有所增强。但价格
回升仍主要集中在上游能源和AI 相关行业，PPI回升的同时，CPI仍受居民消
费需求偏弱影响，整体处于低位。这表明上游涨价向下游商品和服务价格的传
导仍不充分，居民收入与消费之间也尚未形成明显的正向循环，价格回暖向内
需扩散仍需时间。预计出口韧性与政策支持仍是四季度 GDP的主要支撑。全
球 AI产业链景气有望带动四季度出口维持较高增速； 同时，5500亿元地方债

结存限额、PSL 利率下调至 1.5%并支持“六张网”建设，以及 8000亿元新
型政策性金融工具持续投放， 有望推动基建项目形成更多实物工作量， 并对制
造业投资形成支撑。 但以旧换新政策的拉动效应有所减弱， 房地产市场仍处于
深度调整阶段，内需恢复仍面临约束。整体来看，四季度经济仍将呈现外需支
撑较强、内需修复偏慢的格局。
 居民消费修复存在滞后性，就业景气保持平稳。从收入来看，2026 年前三季
度全国居民人均可支配收入同比名义增长 5.3%，扣除价格因素后实际增长
4.4%，增速较上半年均有所回升， 居民收入增长有所改善。从消费支出看，前
三季度全国居民人均消费支出同比名义增长3.6%，增速较上半年回落 0.1 个
百分点，实际增长 2.7%，与上半年持平，收入增速回升尚未同步转化为消费
增长， 居民消费修复节奏有所滞后。从消费结构看，前三季度食品烟酒、 居住、
医疗保健消费支出同比增速分别回升至4.47%、1.89%和 2.39%，其余消费支
出细项增速均较上半年边际回落， 反映消费支出的边际改善主要集中于部分日

常生活及刚性支出，其他消费领域的增长动能仍显不足，居民消费修复的广泛
性有待增强。就业方面，前三季度全国城镇调查失业率平均值为5.21%，较上
半年回升 0.01 个百分点，9 月全国城镇调查失业率为5.2%，环比下降 0.1 个
百分点， 短期就业状况有所改善， 但前三季度整体就业形势尚未出现明显变化。
10 月 10 日，国新办发布会相关负责人介绍， “十五五”时期将加大投资于人
力度，力争在此期间开展补贴性职业技能培训5000 万人次，新增取得技能证
书 4000 万人次、其中取得高级工以上技能证书1400 万人次。稳就业、促增
收与职业技能培训政策持续落地，居民就业稳定性及收入预期有望边际改善，
但考虑到前三季度收入增速回升而消费支出增速未同步加快， 居民消费倾向的
改善仍有待观察， 全年消费增长或延续温和修复态势。就业方面，预计城镇调
查失业率总体保持平稳， 后续就业政策对重点群体就业和劳动者技能提升的支
持力度有望进一步加大，但就业结构性矛盾的缓解仍需一定时间。
 单月工业生产边际改善，AI中间品强劲而消费电子承压。2026年前三季度，

全国规模以上工业增加值同比增长5.3%，增速与 1-8 月持平。9 月单月，工业
增加值同比增长 5.5%，较8 月提高 0.3 个百分点，符合 wind 一致预期 （5.5%）。
工业生产动能有所修复， 但整体增速尚未明显加快。前三季度高技术产业工业
增加值累计同比增长14.3%，较 1-8 月提升 0.1 个百分点；装备制造业工业增
加值累计同比增长 10.2%，均明显高于规上工业整体增速， 高技术相关产业仍
是工业增长的主要支撑。分三大门类看，9 月单月，制造业增加值同比增长
6.0%，较 8 月回落 0.1 个百分点； 电力、热力、燃气及水生产和供应业同比增
长 5.6%，采矿业同比由 8 月的下降转为上升 1.3%，增速均边际回升。三大门
类生产表现有所分化， 工业生产的边际改善尚未体现为各领域同步加速。从细
分行业看，9 月单月，计算机、 通信和其他电子设备制造业、专用设备制造业，
以及铁路、 船舶、 航空航天和其他运输设备制造业同比分别增长16.4%、12.1%、
11.7%，在主要行业中表现靠前。这反映 AI 电子链、 设备更新相关产业及船舶

航空等领域生产较为活跃，工业增长对高技术中间品、资本品及部分出口链条
的依赖度有所上升。石油和天然气开采业同比回升 3.6 个百分点至 9.8%，回
升幅度居前。通用设备制造业、电气机械及器材制造业、汽车制造业等制造业

3
9 月增速保持在 6%以上，但增速相比 8 月边际回落。分产品看，9 月单月，
太阳能发电量（+19.5%） 、工业机器人（+18.9%） 、集成电路（+18.4%）、新
能源汽车（+16.9%）产量同比增速居前，反映新能源、AI 相关中间品及工业
自动化产品仍保持较快增长。但除太阳能发电量同比边际回升外， 上述其余三
个品类增速相比 8 月均边际回落，部分高景气产品的生产扩张速度有所放缓。
与此同时，服务机器人（-17.8%） 、水电（-18.1%） 、智能手机（-26.6%） 、移
动通信手持机 （-26.9%） 产量同比增速均处于深度负增区间， 且相比8 月增速
均边际回落。整体来看，9月工业生产结构性分化延续，AI相关中间品、 资本
品和工业自动化表现较强， 消费电子终端需求仍偏弱。 全年规上工业增加值累
计增速落在 5.2%-5.4%之间， 高技术制造业、 装备制造业及出口仍是主要支撑，
但内需修复偏缓或制约增长动能进一步扩散。
 整体基建投资持续承压，新动能与新基建是结构性亮点。2026 年前三季度，

全国固定资产投资（不含农户）同比下降 7.2%，与 1-8 月增速持平。扣除房
地产开发的固定资产投资下降 4.2%，地产端仍是拖累整体投资的重要因素。
其中，知识产权产品投资同比增长9.3%。高技术产业投资同比增长4.9%，其
中信息服务业，航空、航天器及设备制造业， 电子及通信设备制造业投资分别
增长 20.2%、12.5%、7.6%，说明新质生产力、数字基础设施、航空水运等方
向仍有扩张。根据历史数据倒推测算，9 月单月， 固定资产投资同比下降9.69%，
降幅较 8 月单月收窄 3.82 个百分点，本轮边际修复或主要来自基建与制造业
端的积极变化。基建投资方面，统计局数据显示，前三季度基础设施投资同比
下降 4.2%，降幅相比 1-8 月扩大 0.2 个百分点。其中，信息传输业、航空运
输业、水上运输业投资分别增长 30.8%、13.8%、13.5%。根据历史数据测算，
前三季度广义基建投资同比下降 6.08%，降幅扩大 0.71 个百分点。9 月单月，
广义基建投资同比下降 10.48%，降幅收窄 4.3 个百分点。基建投资单月边际

改善可能来自低基数、天气扰动消退、专项债集中发行后项目资金到位。从资
金端看，截至 10 月 16 日，2026年超长期特别国债 1.3万亿元已经发行完毕，
与去年进度基本持平。9 月单月，地方政府新增专项债发行6204 亿元，同比
增速扩大至 50.18%，截至 9 月末发行进度 80.62%，略慢于去年同期。截至
10 月 16 日，今年地方政府专项债已发行 3.65 万亿元，年内剩余发行额度为
7546 亿元。在低基数、 天气扰动消退，以及剩余专项债、5500亿元地方债务
结存限额和 8000 亿元新型政策性金融工具加快落地形成实物工作量的支撑
下，年内投资单月降幅有望继续收窄。但资金到实物工作量有时滞、冬季施工
约束仍在，全年投资增速或将大幅收窄降幅、靠近零增长区间， 出现明显正增
的概率不大。
 地产市场仍处调整阶段，投资和销售继续走弱。2026 年前三季度，全国房地
产开发投资累计同比下降20.6%，降幅较 1-8 月扩大 0.7个百分点。根据历史
数据倒推测算，9 月单月，房地产开发投资同比下降27.19%，降幅相比 8 月

扩大 1.05 个百分点，说明房企开发活动仍然偏弱，房地产投资对整体投资的
拖累继续存在。供给端，相比于 1-8 月，房屋施工面积同比下降 12.8%，降幅
与上月持平；新开工面积和竣工面积同比降幅分别扩大至 24.9%和 25.1%；需
求端， 相比于 1-8 月，商品房销售面积和销售额降幅分别 扩大至 13.2%和
13.9%。资金端，前三季度房企到位资金同比下降 21.6%，降幅扩大 0.6个百
分点。其中国内贷款下降 33.1%，是各项来源中萎缩最快的一项，外部融资持
续承压、销售回款同步萎缩，从而制约房企资本开支。价格端，9 月单月，70
个大中城市中，一线城市商品住宅销售价格环比继续上涨， 二三线城市环比降
幅总体收窄，一二三线城市同比降幅均收窄，环比上涨城市个数比上月增加，
价格端的结构性改善与总量收缩并存。1-9 月二手房网签面积 6.17亿平方米，
同比增长 10.7%，已超过同期新建商品房销售面积5.65 亿平方米，需求载体
向二手房切换的特征进一步显现。政策方面，财政自 10 月 1 日起对符合条

件的新发放首套住房商业性个人住房贷款按 本金给予年化 1 个百分点、最
长 5 年的贴息。这是财政首次将贴息工具直接用于居民购房贷款，标志着
政策着力点正式由企业和投资端向居民终端需求延伸。 展望四季度， 购房贴
息叠加低基数效应， 有望推动商品房销售同比降幅收窄， 但销售趋势性回暖仍

### Page 4

请务必阅读正文后的重要声明部分
4
需观察。 投资端受房企销售回款偏弱、 现房销售改革可能拉长资金回笼周期等
因素制约，预计全年房地产开发投资同比下降 18%-21%，投资恢复仍然取决
于销售回暖、库存去化和房企现金流改善。
 制造业投资降幅收窄，结构性修复特征突出。2026 年前三季度，制造业投资
累计同比下降 2.1%，降幅较 1-8 月收窄 0.2 个百分点，增速在连续五个月边
际下降后边际回升。根据历史数据倒推测算，9 月单月，制造业投资同比下降
0.95%，降幅相比上月收窄5.11个百分点。9 月投资降幅收窄或受前期需求集
中释放和去年同期基数较低的影响。分行业看，13 个主要行业中，铁路、船
舶、航空航天和其他运输设备制造业投资增速最高，前三季度投资同比增速为
15.8%，计算机、通信和其他电子设备制造业（+9.6%）、纺织业（+4.2%）增
速居前， 电气机械及器材制造业（+1.2%）、 通用设备制造业（+1.1%）同比亦
实现正增长，且增速相比1-8 月边际回升。但需要注意，中游装备制造业投资

的改善， 主要受到出口需求和设备更新政策的带动。 设备工器具购置增速已从
一季度的 13.9%回落至前三季度的 9.7%，说明“两新”政策的拉动作用正在
减弱。金属制品业（-10.1%） 、食品制造业（-10.3%）仍处于深度收缩区间，
是制造业投资的主要拖累因素，主要由于地产及消费链投资内生动能偏弱。预
计四季度制造业投资单月增速将在零附近波动，累计降幅有望进一步收窄至
1.5%-2.0%，但全年累计增速转正的难度仍较大。分行业看，航空航天、电子
设备等行业 有望继续支撑制造业投资，制造业投资的分化格局可能延续。
 高基数与中秋错期抬升读数，消费仍处于低位磨底阶段。2026 年前三季度，
全国社会消费品零售总额同比增长 1.1%，与 1-8 月增速持平，除汽车以外的
消费品零售额同比涨幅扩大 0.1 个百分点至 2.8%，说明汽车消费仍为消费增
速的拖累点。根据统计局披露， 前三季度社会消费商品和服务零售总额同比增
长 2.6%，其中服务零售额增长5.0%，增速相比 1-8 月加快 0.1 个百分点，商

品零售额增长 1.0%，增速持平 1-8 月表现。服务零售中，通讯信息服务类、
旅游咨询租赁服务类、文体休闲服务类零售额较快增长。9 月单月，社会消费
品零售总额同比涨幅扩大 0.7 个百分点至 1.1%，主要受中秋错期叠加低基数
影响。其中，9 月餐饮收入同比涨幅扩大 1 个百分点至 2.1%，商品零售同比
扩大 0.7 个百分点至 1%。从具体类别看，9 月单月主要类别商品零售同比增
速均边际回升。其中，通讯器材类、烟酒类同比分别增长 31.1%、13.8%，相
比 8 月增速分别回升 3.8、1.3 个百分点。通讯器材高增，主要由于手机新品在
9 月集中发布拉动换机需求释放，同时全年 2500 亿元以旧换新资金在 9 月全
部下达，换机需求提前兑现。家电和汽车是仅有的两类增速回落品类：家电类
零售额同比增长1.9%， 增速较8 月回落 0.4个百分点； 汽车类同比下降21.2%，
降幅扩大 2.7个百分点。汽车类消费收缩，主要由于去年四季度前多地以旧换
新补贴额度接近用尽，消费者集中购车，推动汽车单月销量创近年同期新高。

今年补贴力度有所收缩， 叠加去年集中购车对部分需求形成提前释放，汽车消
费增长因此承压。此外，石油及制品类、体育娱乐用品类、家具类、建筑及装
潢材料类、金银珠宝类等前期累计增速回落的品类，9 月单月增速边际回升幅
度均超 2 个百分点。该回暖主要依托中秋错期、低基数效应、价格因素与季节
性窗口。整体来看， 年内消费有望保持低位企稳， 趋势性回升的动力仍显不足。
四季度低基数效应有望带动社零增速小幅回升， 但汽车消费面临去年同期高基
数、以旧换新补贴力度减弱等压力，低基数带来的提振作用或较为有限。
 风险提示：政策落地节奏不及预期风险，海外经济超预期波动风险。已公布的
宏观或产业政策在实际执行过程中，可能因配套措施不完善、部门协调不畅、
地方执行力度差异或市场环境变化等因素，导致其推进速度、覆盖范围或最终
效果慢于或弱于市场预期，从而影响政策目标的实现与经济修复的进程； 主要
经济体的政治局势、经济数据或宏观政策出现意外变化，其幅度或频率超出市
场普遍预测。这种超预期波动可能引发全球金融市场动荡、 贸易条件恶化或外

需急剧收缩，并通过金融、贸易及预期渠道向国内传导，增加我国外部环境的
复杂性与不稳定性。

分析师承诺  
本报告署名分析师具有中国证券业协会授予的证券投资咨询执业资格并注册为证券分析师，报告所采用的数据均
来自合法合规渠道，分析逻辑基于分析师的职业理解，通过合理判断得出结论，独立、客观地出具本报告。分析师承
诺不曾因，不因，也将不会因本报告中的具体推荐意见或观点而直接或间接获取任何形式的补偿。

投资评级说明  
报告中投资建议所涉及的评级分为公司评级和行业评级（另有说明的除外） 。评级标准为报告发布日后 6 个月内
的相对市场表现，即：以报告发布日后 6 个月内公司股价（或行业指数）相对同期相关证券市场代表性指数的涨跌幅
作为基准。其中：A 股市场以沪深 300 指数为基准， 新三板市场以三板成指 （针对协议转让标的） 或三板做市指数 （针
对做市转让标的）为基准；香港市场以恒生指数为基准；美国市场以纳斯达克综合指数或标普 500 指数为基准。  
公司评级  
买入：未来 6 个月内，个股相对同期相关证券市场代表性指数涨幅在 20%以上  
持有：未来 6 个月内，个股相对同期相关证券市场代表性指数涨幅介于 10%与 20%之间 
中性：未来 6 个月内，个股相对同期相关证券市场代表性指数涨幅介于 -10%与 10%之间  
回避：未来 6 个月内，个股相对同期相关证券市场代表性指数涨幅介于 -20%与-10%之间  
卖出：未来 6 个月内，个股相对同期相关证券市场代表性指数涨幅在 -20%以下  
行业评级  
强于大市：未来 6 个月内，行业整体回报高于同期相关证券市场代表性指数 5%以上 
跟随大市：未来 6 个月内，行业整体回报介于同期相关证券市场代表性指数 -5%与 5%之间 
弱于大市：未来 6 个月内，行业整体回报低于同期相关证券市场代表性指数 -5%以下

### Page 6

Wiwynn (6669.TW)
19 October 2026 Citi Research
6
Wiwynn
Company description
Established in April 2012 and listed in March 2019, Wiwynn is one of the
leading cloud infrastructure providers focusing on servers, storage, and rack
solutions for global CSPs' data centers. Key customers include Microsoft,
Meta Platforms, and Amazon, which together accounted for >90% of sales
with tier-2 CSPs accounting for single-digit percentage of sales. Wiwynn
used to be running at an asset-light business model but has expanded

capacity for server motherboard manufacturing and rack final assembly on its
own in recent years, increasing in-house supply ratio.
Investment strategy
We rate Wiwynn as Buy. We expect the secular growth in global data center
and cloud-based IP traffic to act as a key driver for Wiwynn in years to come.
We expect Wiwynn to benefit from data center growth amid rising ODM
Direct/white-box server adoption. With order momentum having started to
recover since 4Q23, we see Wiwynn's growth being backed by continuous

rollouts of data centers from existing customers and new project wins from
Amazon.
Valuation
Our target price for Wiwynn is NT$3,200. We value Wiwynn based on PE as
we believe the company’s earnings performance and industry dynamics will
be the key focus areas for the market. Our TP is based on 16x 2027E EPS,
which is at the top end of the historical trading range since listing and above
the average PE multiple of the peer group. We believe our target multiple is

justified by the company’s 100% exposure to data centers, rising scale, and
robust earnings growth, as well as stabilizing ROE.
Risks
Key downside risks that could cause Wiwynn’s shares to trade below our
target price include: 1) Potential volatility in capex deployment; 2) Component
price hike; 3) Peer competition; and 4) FX volatility.

Guide to Citi Research Fundamental Research Investment Ratings:
Citi Research stock recommendations include an investment rating and an optional risk rating to highlight high risk stocks.

Risk rating takes into account both price volatility and fundamental criteria. Stocks will either have no risk rating or a High risk
rating assigned.
Investment Ratings: Citi Research investment ratings are Buy, Neutral and Sell. Our ratings are a function of analyst
expectations of expected total return ("ETR") and risk. ETR is the sum of the forecast price appreciation (or depreciation) plus
the dividend yield for a stock within the next 12 months.  The target price is based on a 12 month time horizon. The Investment

rating definitions are: Buy (1) ETR of 15% or more or 25% or more for High risk stocks; and Sell (3) for negative ETR. Any covered
stock not assigned a Buy or a Sell is a Neutral (2). For stocks rated Neutral (2), if an analyst believes that there are insufficient
valuation drivers and/or investment catalysts to derive a positive or negative investment view, they may elect with the approval
of Citi Research management not to assign a target price and, thus, not derive an ETR. Citi Research may suspend its rating

and target price and assign “Rating Suspended” status for regulatory and/or internal policy reasons. Citi Research may also
suspend its rating and target price and assign "Under Review" status for other exceptional circumstances (e.g. lack of
information critical to the analyst's thesis, trading suspension) affecting the company and/or trading in the company's
securities. In both such situations, the rating and target price will show as “—“ and “-“ respectively in the rating history price

chart. Prior to 11 April 2022 Citi Research assigned “Under Review” status to both situations and prior to 11 Nov 2020 only in
exceptional circumstances. As soon as practically possible, the analyst will publish a note re-establishing a rating and
investment thesis. Investment ratings are determined by the ranges described above at the time of initiation of coverage, a
change in investment and/or risk rating, or a change in target price (subject to limited management discretion). At times, the

expected total returns may fall outside of these ranges because of market price movements and/or other short-term volatility
or trading patterns. Such interim deviations will be permitted but will become subject to review by Research Management.
Your decision to buy or sell a security should be based upon your personal investment objectives and should be made only
after evaluating the stock's expected performance and risk.
Catalyst Watch/Short Term Views (“STV”) Ratings Disclosure:

Catalyst Watch and STV Upside/Downside calls: Citi Research may also include a Catalyst Watch or STV Upside or Downside
call to indicate the analyst expects the share price to rise (fall) in absolute terms over a specified period of 30 or 90 days in
reaction to one or more specific near-term catalysts or events impacting the company or the market. A Catalyst Watch will be
published when Analyst confidence is high that an impact to share price will occur; it will be a STV when confidence level is

moderate. A Catalyst Watch or STV Upside/Downside call will automatically expire at the end of the specified 30/90 day
period. The Catalyst Watch will also be automatically removed if share price performance (calculated at market close) exceeds
15% against the direction of the call (unless over-ridden by the analyst). The analyst may also remove a Catalyst Watch or STV
call prior to the end of the specified period in a published research note. A Catalyst Watch/STV Upside or Downside call may be

時碩 (4566  TT)  Trading Buy

2H26 營運明顯增長 ，投資建議 Trading Buy 。  
投 資 建 議 Trading Buy ： 雖 目 前 時碩 (4566 TT) 處於 CB 緘 默 期 ，
未 提 供 明 確 展 望 ， 但 受 惠 於 航 太 、 散 熱 產 品 出 貨 增 長 ，3Q26 營
運 已 明 顯 攀 升 。 考 量 2026 、 2027 年 營 運 動 能 向 上 ， 投 資 建 議
Trading Buy ， 目 標 價 92 元 (以 202 7 年 EPS 及 PE  17 倍 評 價 )。  
時 碩 積 極 布 局 高 成 長 潛 力 的 利 基 型 市 場 ， 包 含 液 冷 散 熱 系 統 、
人 形 機 器 人 零 組 件 ： 液冷散熱 方面， 時 碩 致 力 於 提 供 一 站 式 的
全 方 位 熱 管 理 解 決 方 案 ， 其 產 品 線 涵 蓋 快 接 頭 、 分 歧 管 、 冷 卻
液 分 配 單 元 、 微 通 道 板 及 技 術 門 檻 較 高 的 水 冷 板 ， 並 能 提 供 不
鏽 鋼 與 銅 等 多 樣 材 質 。公司 已 成 功 獲 得 美 、 日 系 液 冷 系 統 大 廠
訂單，且於 2H26 放量 出貨 ， 預估 散 熱 產 品 佔 202 6 年 營 收 比 重
有望 接近 10% 。 人 形 機 器 人方 面 ， 時 碩 積 極 拓 展 此 領 域 的 關 鍵
零 組 件 業 務 ， 產 品 涵 蓋 諧 波 減 速 機 關 鍵 零 組 件 、 機 器 手 臂 關 節
精 密 機 械 加 工 零 件 。 時 碩 目 前 已 打 入 美 國 與 中 國 廠 商 的 供 應 鏈
體 系 ； 中 國 無 錫 廠 藉 由 地 緣 優 勢 積 極 切 入 關 節 零 件 製 造 。 泰 國
春 武 里 新 廠也 規 劃 將 產 線 逐 步 延 伸 至 機 器 人 相 關 應 用 領 域 。 
3Q26 營 收 明 顯 躍 升，預估 2026 、2027 年 營 運 動 能 向 上：雖 1H26
獲 利 表 現 平 平 ， 但 受 惠 於 航 太 、 散 熱 應 用 出 貨 明 顯 增 長 帶 動 ，
3Q26 營收 18.18 億元， QoQ+23.8 6%、 YoY+43.23% ； 且 出 貨 動
能 有 望 延 伸 至 4Q26 。2026 年 方 面 ，汽 車 、 航 太 、 工 業 三 大 領 域
皆有望較 2025 年 增 長。汽 車 應 用 方 面， 雖 中 國 市 場 競 爭 仍 然 激
烈，但 因 切 入 新 能 源 車 領 域 且 客 戶 需 求 帶 動 下，營 收穩 定 增 長。
航太領域 方面， 天 陽 航 太 已 通 過 多 項 航 太 認 證 ， 成 功 打 入 全 球
前 兩 大 飛 機 製 造 商 及 Tier 1 供 應 商 ， 預 估 營 收 將維 持 高 速 成 長
態勢 。 工 業 應 用 方 面 ， 既 有 領 域 有 望 較2025 年 改 善 ， 且 新 成 長
動 能 將 來 自 於 液 冷 散 熱 相 關 產 品 及 人 形 機 器 人 零 組 件 。 預 估
2026 年 營 收 66.00 億 元 ，YoY+ 32.62 %， 毛 利 率 維 持 高 檔 ， 營 業
利益 YoY+ 97.15 %。 預 估 稅 後 純 益 3.71 億 元 ， YoY+ 148.83 %，
EPS 3.36 元。 隨 著 航 太 、 散 熱 應 用 持 續 發 酵 ， 且 泰 國 新 廠 建 置
完 成 ， 預 估 2027 年 營 收 、 獲 利 動 能 向 上 。

  時碩 (4566  TT) E SG ：

推 動 委 員 會 」 負 責 執 行 與 追 蹤，將 氣 候 變 遷 及 各 項 永 續 議 題 納 入 公 司 整 體 策
略 與 風 險 管 理 流 程 中。在 環 境 面，公 司 依 循 國 際 標 準 進 行 溫 室 氣 體 盤 查 與 第
三 方 確 信 ， 並 透 過 節 能 設 備 導 入 、 能 源 管 理 與 監 測 系 統 、 製 程 優 化 、 太 陽 能
及綠電等多元措施推動減碳與能源效率提升，同時強化水資源與廢棄物管
理 ， 藉 由 回 收 再 利 用 、 廢 水 循 環 及 資 源 化 設 備 ， 落 實 減 量 及 循 環 經 濟 ； 原 物
料 與 包 材 則 遵 循 RoHS 、REACH 等 規 範，降 低 製 程 對 環 境 的 衝 擊。社 會 面 方
面，時 碩 重 視 員 工 薪 資 福 利、培 訓 與 職 涯 發 展，推 動 性 別 平 等 與 管 理 階 層 多
元 化，並 建 立 職 業 安 全 衛 生 制 度 及 教 育 訓 練，以 降 低 職 災 風 險；在 供 應 鏈 則
透 過 定 期 稽 核 與 評 分 機 制，提 升 供 應 商 在 品 質、交 期 與 服 務 上 的 表 現，並 以
產 品 品 質 與 安 全 為 核 心 ， 確 保 生 產 與 產 品 符 合 相 關 要 求 。 同 時 ， 公 司 以「 企
業 公 民 」 自 我 定 位，投 入 社 會 公 益 與 在 地 關 懷，減 少 營 運 對 社 區 的 負 面 影 響，
並 維 持 完 善 的 資 訊 安 全 管 理 以 避 免 資 訊 外 洩。在 治 理 面，時 碩 建 構 包 含 獨 立
董 事 與 女 性 董 事 的 董 事 會 結 構，設 置 薪 酬 與 審 計 等 功 能 性 委 員 會，訂 定 涵 蓋
營 運 、 財 務、 法 遵 、 環 境 與 氣 候 變 遷 、 人 資 與 人 權 等 面 向 的 風 險 管 理 政 策 ，
並 透 過 法 說 會、網 站 與 永 續 報 告 與 利 害 關 係 人溝 通，同 時 積 極 參 與 各 項 ESG
獎 項 評 選 ， 以 強 化 公 司 治 理 與 永 續 形 象 。

6 群益投顧 
  【 投 資 評 等 說 明 】 
  評等  定義  
  強力買進 (Strong Buy)  首 次 評 等 潛 在 上 漲 空 間≥ 35%  
  買進 (Buy)  15%≤ 首 次 評 等 潛 在 上 漲 空 間<35%  
  區間操作 (Trading Buy)  5%≤ 首 次 評 等 潛 在 上 漲 空 間<15 %

中立 (Neutral)  
無 法 由 基 本 面 給 予 投 資 評 等 
預 期 近 期 股 價 將 處 於 盤 整 
建 議 降 低 持 股

【 投 資 評 等 調 整 機 制 】 
  投 資 評 等 係「 首 次 」給 予 特 定 個 股 投 資 評 等 時，其 前 一 日 收 盤 價 相 較3 個月
目 標 價 之 潛 在 上 漲 空 間 計 算 而 得 。 個 股 投 資 評 等 分 為四 個 等 級 ， 定 義 如 上 。
爾 後 的 投 資 評 等係 依 循 「 首 次 評 等 」 ， 直 到 停 止 推 薦 。  
「 強 力 買 進 」、「 買 進 」及 「 區 間 操 作 」均有 upside 目 標 價 。差 別 僅 在 於 ，
首 次 評 等 時 潛 在 報 酬 率 不 同 。 「 中 立 」 則 無 目 標 價 。 
一 旦 我 們 給 予 特 定 個 股「 強 力 買 進 」、「 買 進 」或「 區 間 操 作 」之 投 資 評 等，
就 是 責 任 的 開 始，爾 後 將 透 過 各 式 研 究 報 告 作 定 期 性、持 續 性 追 蹤 基 本 面 及
股 價 變 化 ， 直 到 停 止 推 薦 。 
停 止 推 薦 情 境 ： 
1.  達 目 標 價 。 
2.  雖 未 達 原 訂 目 標 價 ， 但 檢 視 基 本 面 、 訊 息 面 、 籌 碼 面 等 多 方 訊 息 ， 研
判 股 價 上 漲 空 間 已 然 有 限 ， 將 適 時 出 具 降 評 報 告 。 
3.  推 薦 後 股 價 不 漲 反 跌 ， 亦 將 出 具 降 評 報 告 。

【免責聲明】  
  本 研 究 報 告 僅 提 供 予 特 定 人 之 客 戶 作 為 參 考 資 料「 非 經 同 意 不 得 轉 載 」 。我
們 並 不 確 保 此 資 訊 的 完 整 性 與 正 確 性，投 資 人 應 了 解，報 告 中 有 關 未 來 預 測
之 陳 述 可 能 不 會 實 現，因 而 不 應 被 依 賴。而 且 此 報 告 並 非 根 據 特 定 投 資 目 的
或 依 預 定 對 象 之 財 務 狀 況 所 撰 寫 出 來 的，因 此，此 研 究 報 告 的 目 的，既 非 對
投 資 人 於 買 賣 證 券、選 擇 權、期 貨 或 其 他 證 券 相 關 之 衍 生 性 商 品 提 供 詢 價 服
務，亦 非 作 為 進 行 交 易 的 要 約。投 資 人 應 注 意 到 相 關 證 券 之 價 值 及 收 益，可
能會有無預警地上升或下降，產生投資回報金額可能比原始投資來得少的
情形。

## Document 227: AI交易裂缝扩大：1%客户撑起Anthropic和OpenAI 80%企业端收入，Token用量仍在涨，实际支出却停滞不前

Source PDF: all report pdf/AI交易裂缝扩大：1%客户撑起Anthropic和OpenAI 80%企业端收入，Token用量仍在涨，实际支出却停滞不前.pdf
Report source: Unknown
Date: 未知日期
Pages: 4
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

AI 商业化叙事遭遇数据 " 打脸 " ： Anthropic 和 OpenAI 的企业端收⼊，有 80% 来⾃仅占客⼾
总量 1% 的头部客⼾， Token ⽤量攀升却美元收⼊停滞， Anthropic 实际 Token 价格半年内腰
斩⾄ 0.83 美元。⾼盛、 Apollo 、 Citadel 相继发出警报 ——" 杰⽂斯悖论 " 未能兑现，付费渗
透率触顶，算⼒投资回报率跌⾄ 8 年最低。 AI 繁荣，究竟是真实需求还是资本⾃我循环？
摘要：
AI ⼤模型的商业化叙事正⾯临数据层⾯的挑战。企业卡巨头 Ramp 追踪逾 7 万家美国企业的⽀付
数据后发现， Anthropic 和 OpenAI 的企业端收⼊，有 80% 来⾃仅占客⼾总量 1% 的头部客⼾，且
这⼀⾼度集中的需求正在触顶 ——Token 使⽤量继续攀升，但实际美元⽀出增⻓已近乎停滞，付
费⽤⼾渗透率也趋于平缓。
Ramp ⾸席经济学家 Ara Kharazian 在其 10 ⽉ AI 指数报告中指出， 8 ⽉和 9 ⽉连续两个⽉ Token ⽀

出 " ⼏乎零增⻓ " ， 10 ⽉初虽勉强创出历史新⾼，却仅⽐ 8 ⽉峰值⾼出 1.5% 。⽀出停滞的核⼼驱动
⼒是价格通缩：前沿模型价格持续下⾏，叠加模型效率提升，每百万 Token 的混合均价不断⾛
低，量价背离格局⽇益明显。
这⼀信号已引发主流机构警觉。⾼盛警告， Token 需求增速必须跑赢价格降幅，才能⽀撑持续的
算⼒投资； Apollo ⾸席经济学家 Torsten Slok 援引 Ramp 数据指出，算⼒需求曲线 " 平坦且对价格
⾼度敏感 " ，企业买家在价格微动时便会压缩⽤量或转向廉价开源替代品。与此同时， Citadel 创
始⼈ Ken Griffin 公开表⽰，市场 " ⼏乎可以肯定 " 正处于 AI 泡沫之中。
需求⾼度集中，鲸⻥客⼾撑起营收
AI 企业⽀出的集中程度在所有软件品类中⾸屈⼀指。 Ramp 数据显⽰，⻛险投资⽀持的初创公司
⼈均 AI ⽉⽀出达 76 美元，是私募股权背景企业的逾 10 倍。
头部集中态势在 Anthropic 尤为突出。据 YipitData ， Anthropic 前 1% 客⼾在 8 ⽉贡献了 46% 的营

收，较⼀年前的 25% ⼏近翻倍。Anthropic 的招股说明书则显⽰，仅两名未披露⾝份的客⼾便贡
献了其 2025 年营收的近四分之⼀。
⾸⻚ 资讯 快讯 ⾏情 ⽇历 APP VIP 会员 ⼤师课 ⻅闻历 搜索

### Page 2

资本流向揭⽰了这⼀⽣态的内部循环： Cursor ⺟公司 Anysphere 去年 11 ⽉完成 23 亿美元融资，
估值 293 亿美元，由 Accel 、 Coatue 联合领投， Thrive Capital 、 Andreessen Horowitz 、英伟达
及⾕歌参与其中；三个⽉后， Coatue 与新加坡 GIC 联合领投 Anthropic 300 亿美元 G 轮融资，英
伟达再度跟投。
Coatue 的资⾦流⼊ Cursor ， Cursor 将其转化为 Claude Token 消耗， Token 消耗计⼊ Anthropic 营
收，⽽ Anthropic 营收⼜⽀撑着 Coatue 所持 Anthropic 股权的估值溢价。
价格战加剧， Anthropic 实际 Token 价格腰斩
模型价格正在快速崩塌。 Ornn Anthropic Token 价格指数 —— 衡量客⼾实际⽀付的每 Token 成本
—— 已从 9 ⽉中旬的逾 1.70 美元跌⾄ 0.83 美元，创该基准 6 ⽉推出以来的历史新低。

即便如此， Anthropic 仍贵于 OpenAI 。 OpenAI 对应指数⽬前为 0.45 美元。 OpenAI 的实际定价在
7 ⽉底⼤幅崩落 —— 彼时其将 GPT-5.6 Luna 和 Terra 模型价格最⾼下调 80% ，时间节点距中国⼤

### Page 3

⻛险提⽰及免责条款
市场有⻛险，投资需谨慎。本⽂不构成个⼈投资建议，也未考虑到个别⽤⼾特殊的投资⽬标、财务状况或需要。⽤⼾应考虑本⽂
中的任何意⻅、观点或结论是否符合其特定状况。据此投资，责任⾃负。

写评论
模型公司 Moonshot 发布 Kimi K3 开源权重仅三天，后者被认为是当⽉引发芯⽚股进⼊熊市的导⽕
索。 OpenAI 此后已倾向将 IPO 推迟⾄ 2027 年。
" 杰⽂斯悖论 " 未能兑现
价格下跌刺激需求爆发、最终推动总收⼊上升 —— 这是 AI 多头惯常援引的 " 杰⽂斯悖论 " 逻辑。然
⽽现有数据并不⽀持这⼀判断。
⾼盛在 9 ⽉ 25 ⽇的报告中指出，基于 Anera 和 OpenRouter 数据，⾃去年 12 ⽉以来 Token 总需求已
增⻓约 18 倍，但对⾼⽑利率前沿模型的需求增速仅为整体的⼀半。价格下⾏带来的利润压缩已
传导⾄数据中⼼投资层。⾼盛测算，亚⻢逊、 Alphabet 、 Meta 、微软和甲⻣⽂五家企业的已实
现投资资本回报率（ ROIC ）已从 40% 降⾄ 25% ，为其数据集⾃ 2016 年以来的最低⽔平。

Torsten Slok 对此直⾔不讳： " 硅⾕的前提假设是算⼒需求⽆限，但近期 Token ⽀出增速的放缓表
明，这种需求或许只在算⼒近乎免费时才存在。 "
他警告，决定数据中⼼建设模式能否成⽴的核⼼变量，既⾮物理容量，也⾮电⼒供给，⽽是 " ⽤
⼾实际愿意为算⼒⽀付的价格 "—— ⽽这⼀价格的⽀撑，有赖于企业能否将 Token 消耗转化为营
收增⻓、利润改善或实质性成本节约，" ⽬前这些效益尚未在规模层⾯显现。未来或许会有，也
或许不会。 "
付费渗透趋于饱和，增⻓需靠深度⽽⾮⼴度
除头部⽀出放缓外，付费⽤⼾基础的扩张同样陷⼊瓶颈。 Ramp 数据显⽰， 9 ⽉企业⽹络中的 AI
付费采⽤率仅上升 0.62 个百分点，⾄ 55.9% 。
Kharazian 在报告中写道： " 感觉我们已经处于 S 曲线的顶端，市场增⻓将不得不依赖更深度的使
⽤，⽽⾮更多的付费⽤⼾。 "
这与 Ramp 产品总监 Adam Sommer 在今年 6 ⽉的表态形成鲜明对⽐ —— 后者曾在纽约⼀场论坛上

表⽰， " 我们在 Ramp 平台上最⼤的 AI ⽀出企业，在过去⼏个⽉中每⽉⽀出翻倍 " 。三个⽉后，前
1% 的头部企业⼈均⽀出已低于 7 ⽉峰值⽔平。

## Document 234: AI算力需求带动PCB设备增长，大族激光Q3营收超80亿元，净利同比大增137%｜财报见闻

Source PDF: all report pdf/AI算力需求带动PCB设备增长，大族激光Q3营收超80亿元，净利同比大增137%｜财报见闻.pdf
Report source: Unknown
Date: 未知日期
Pages: 3
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

⼤族激光第三季度营收 80.61 亿元，同⽐增⻓ 58.07% ；归⺟净利润 8.89 亿元，同⽐增⻓
137.03% 。前三季度营收增⻓ 68.92% ，扣⾮净利润同⽐增⻓ 304.87% ，盈利增⻓主要来⾃
主营业务。同期，经营活动现⾦流净额转正⾄ 2.27 亿元，但应收账款增⾄ 132.01 亿元、存
货升⾄ 77.48 亿元。
摘要：
⼤族激光 2026 年前三季度营收和净利润均实现较快增⻓。财报显⽰，公司前三季度实现营业收
⼊ 214.74 亿元，同⽐增⻓ 68.92% ；归属于上市公司股东的净利润为 21.77 亿元，同⽐增⻓
152.19% 。第三季度单季营收 80.61 亿元、净利润 8.89 亿元，同⽐分别增⻓ 58.07% 和 137.03% ，
延续了上半年的增⻓势头。
盈利指标⽅⾯，前三季度扣除⾮经常性损益后的净利润为 23.01 亿元，同⽐增⻓ 304.87% ，⾼于
归⺟净利润增速。同期，公司基本每股收益为 2.11 元，上年同期为 0.84 元；加权平均净资产收益

率为 10.39% ，同⽐提⾼ 5.37 个百分点。
报告期内，⼤族激光⼦公司⼤族数控在⾹港联交所主板上市，募集资⾦近 50 亿元。受此影响，
公司少数股东权益由年初的 16.43 亿元增⾄ 40.87 亿元，总资产则由 382 亿元增⾄ 508.50 亿元。

### Page 2

营收与利润：收⼊增⻓带动盈利提升
前三季度，⼤族激光实现营业收⼊ 214.74 亿元，较上年同期的 127.13 亿元增加 87.61 亿元，同⽐
增⻓ 68.92% 。同期，公司营业利润为 29.77 亿元，同⽐增⻓ 189.57% ；利润总额为 29.83 亿元，
同⽐增⻓约 190% 。
从利润构成看，前三季度扣⾮净利润同⽐增⻓ 304.87% ，⾼于归⺟净利润 152.19% 的增幅，表明
扣除⾮经常性损益后，公司盈利增⻓仍较为明显。报告期内，公司⾮经常性损益合计约为 -1.24
亿元，其中持有⾦融资产公允价值变动带来约 2.02 亿元损失，对当期利润形成⼀定拖累。相⽐之
下，上年同期相关公允价值变动为正贡献，影响了两期净利润增速的可⽐性。
成本⽅⾯，前三季度营业成本为 138.47 亿元，同⽐增⻓ 61.53% ，低于营业收⼊增速。销售费
⽤、管理费⽤和研发费⽤分别为 12.84 亿元、 12.21 亿元和 18.36 亿元，合计约 43.41 亿元。相关
费⽤增速低于收⼊增速，在⼀定程度上反映出收⼊规模扩⼤后费⽤占⽐有所下降，但后续仍需

结合⽑利率、费⽤率及业务结构变化判断盈利改善的持续性。
现⾦流：经营活动现⾦流转正，投资⽀出增加
前三季度，公司经营活动产⽣的现⾦流量净额为 2.27 亿元，上年同期为净流出 8.62 亿元，同⽐改
善约 10.89 亿元。同期，销售商品、提供劳务收到的现⾦为 161.75 亿元，同⽐增⻓约 57% ，经营
活动现⾦流较上年同期有所改善。
不过，经营活动现⾦流净额仍明显低于同期归⺟净利润，反映出利润与现⾦回收之间仍存在差
距。随着收⼊规模扩⼤，应收账款同步增⻓，后续回款情况仍值得关注。
投资活动⽅⾯，公司前三季度现⾦流量净额为 -13.98 亿元，上年同期为净流⼊ 7.58 亿元。现⾦流
变化与股权投资及固定资产投⼊增加有关，相关投资涉及安腾创新、永通智造、胜宏科技、景
旺电⼦等企业，以及华东区域总部基地建设。
筹资活动现⾦流量净额为 37.85 亿元，主要受⼤族数控赴港上市募资等因素影响。期末货币资⾦
余额为 98.82 亿元，较年初增加 18.69 亿元。需要注意的是，⼦公司上市募资属于资本性资⾦来

源，与主营业务经营产⽣的现⾦流性质不同。
资产负债表：规模扩张，应收账款与存货同步增⻓
截⾄报告期末，⼤族激光总资产增⾄ 508.50 亿元，较年初增⻓ 32.95% 。除经营规模扩⼤外，⼤
族数控上市带来的资⾦注⼊及少数股东权益增加，也是资产规模扩张的重要因素。

### Page 3

⻛险提⽰及免责条款
市场有⻛险，投资需谨慎。本⽂不构成个⼈投资建议，也未考虑到个别⽤⼾特殊的投资⽬标、财务状况或需要。⽤⼾应考虑本⽂
中的任何意⻅、观点或结论是否符合其特定状况。据此投资，责任⾃负。

图⽚
应收账款由年初的 83.24 亿元增⾄ 132.01 亿元，增⻓ 58.59% ；存货由 52.22 亿元增⾄ 77.48 亿
元，增⻓ 48.37% 。两项资产均出现较⼤幅度增⻓，但增速低于同期营收增速的判断仍需结合收
⼊确认、账期及业务结构等因素综合分析。期末应收账款约占总资产的 26% ，其回收进度将影
响后续现⾦流表现。
预付款项由 2.35 亿元增⾄ 8.08 亿元，增幅约 243.56% ；合同负债由 11.06 亿元增⾄ 22.41 亿元，增
⻓ 102.66% 。合同负债增加反映公司预收款项有所增⻓，但其后续转化为收⼊的节奏仍取决于
订单交付及验收进度。
前三季度，公司计提信⽤减值损失约 1.50 亿元，同⽐增⻓ 75.14% 。在应收账款规模扩⼤的背景

下，信⽤减值损失的变化值得持续跟踪。
负债⽅⾯，短期借款由年初的 9.11 亿元增⾄ 18.79 亿元，⻓期借款则由 26.35 亿元降⾄ 12.87 亿
元，借款期限结构有所变化。期末资产负债率约为 48.2% ，低于年初的 50.5% 。不过，判断财务
结构变化仍需综合考察有息负债规模、偿债期限及经营现⾦流状况。

Economics | 3

### Page 4

Monthly Economics
Sector Analysis
Personal Consumption Expenditures (PCE)
• We've lifted our Q3 expectation for real PCE on incoming data and a slightly firmer consumer
backdrop in the wake of recent revisions.
Consumer spending looks firmer in the third quarter, and we now expect real PCE to rise at a 3.4%
annualized clip. This upward revision reflects recent data, with August consumption coming in firm
with real spending rising by the fastest monthly clip in nearly two years, but also reflects a slightly

firmer consumer backdrop in the wake of the BEA's annual revisions released late September. Although
revisions did not materially alter spending, they lifted the level of household income in recent years,
which also boosted the personal saving rate. The trajectory of real income growth remains weak, but
the consumer still looks to be in a slightly stronger financial position than previously believed, which
helps explain recent resilience in spending and suggests consumers may have a bit more runway ahead.

Investment: Equipment, Intellectual Property Products and Inventories
• The AI build out will lead to another surge in business fixed investment spending in Q3, though the
effect on headline growth will be partly offset by stronger imports.
Business fixed investment spending continues to be supported primarily by the high-tech buildout.
We forecast real equipment investment to rise at around a 16% annualized clip in Q3, but part of
this boost to headline GDP growth will be offset by stronger import growth that is facilitating the

build out. Net imports of computers are tracking to surge in the third quarter, which will provide a
sizable lift to information processing equipment investment, or around half of overall equipment.
And while non-defense capital goods shipments (including aircraft) slipped in August, the near-term
growth trajectory remains supportive of a pickup in equipment. The rebuild in inventories we have
been anticipating is also tracking to be a bit stronger as businesses replenish drawn down product.

Investment: Residential
• The abrupt rise in mortgage rates is likely to dampen residential investment over the next few
quarters. Residential spending should find support next year as longer-term interest rates
moderate somewhat.
We are unlikely to see another positive residential investment reading in Q3. Rising mortgage rates

have sent a chill over builder outlooks, pushing sales expectations to their weakest level in more than
three years. While builders have responded with deeper price cuts, affordability pressures continue
to weigh on single-family sales and construction outlays. Apartment construction is simultaneously
soft amid rising financing costs and modest rent growth, and home improvement spending remains
constrained by high materials costs and interest rates. While residential investment is likely to remain

4 | Economics

### Page 5

Economic Outlook: October 2026 Economics
a drag this year, we expect a slight moderation in longer-term interest rates in 2027, which would
provide a more supportive backdrop.
Investment: Nonresidential Structures
• We have not made material changes to our nonresidential structures forecast. Despite elevated
interest rates, data center and related spending should continue to support structures investment
through our forecast horizon.
Nonresidential structures spending has accelerated sharply, overwhelmingly driven by data centers

and electric power facilities. As data center construction expands geographically and demand rises
for semiconductors, high-tech capex has also started to generate positive spillovers for warehousing
and manufacturing construction. This buildout, which appears relatively insensitive to interest rates,
should be enough to sustain solid growth in structures investment even as spending across most
other categories remains essentially flat. Domestic energy is another green shoot, with elevated prices

supporting ongoing growth in active rotary rigs.

Net Exports & Trade
• An expected drag from trade in Q3 got larger in light of a near-record trade deficit in August, net
exports is now expected to lop off three full percentage points from third quarter growth.

Exports increased in August for the first time in four months, but the increase was more than offset by
an even larger increase in imports. The upshot was a widening in the trade deficit to a near-record gap
of more than $105 billion. Going into the report, we had a 1.9 percentage point drag from net exports
penciled in for Q3 GDP; we revised that to a full 3.0 percentage points drag in light of the record gap.
Even a modest narrowing in the final months of the year would likely mean a lift to headline GDP in

Q4, but the real story here is that trade will remain a headwind throughout the forecast period owing
largely to surging imports of tech-related products amid the AI boom.
Labor Market
• The jobs market remains stable. We continue to expect the unemployment rate to end the year at
4.2% and hold near that level in 2027.
We modestly revised down our near-term payroll growth forecast following the September jobs
report, but otherwise we have left our labor market outlook unchanged. Measures of labor demand

growth continue to point to a stable labor market. Job openings have inched higher on a year-ago
basis but remain broadly steady while initial jobless claims, WARN notices and Challenger job cut
announcements continue to signal low layoffs. Labor force participation appears to be improving after
its sharp pullback in the first half of the year. Looking ahead, we expect payroll growth to average
around 60K per month in 2027, roughly in line with our estimate of breakeven job growth. That should

Economics | 5

### Page 6

Monthly Economics
keep the unemployment rate near 4.2% in 2027 and leave labor market conditions broadly consistent
with "full employment." Notably, our expectation for labor cost growth of ~3.4% is the reason we
continue to reiterate that labor cost growth is not a contributor to the excess inflation seen in the
data.
Inflation
• We expect the core PCE deflator to rise 3.2% and 2.6% Q4/Q4 in 2026 and 2027, respectively.
Headline inflation remains heavily influenced by developments in the Middle East, with higher oil

prices lifting gasoline and diesel prices. Food inflation has been tame this year but looks poised to
strengthen as higher transportation and commodity prices work their way through supply chains.
Beyond food and energy, price pressures remain concentrated in a handful of categories, most notably
airfares and AI-related tech goods. Core goods inflation is showing signs of stabilizing, as waning tariff-
related price pressures have been offset by resilient demand tied to the AI buildout. We expect some

improvement here in 2027. Still-low shelter inflation and cooler price growth for airfares and health
care should help bring about some moderation in core services inflation next year. We expect the core
PCE deflator to rise 3.2% on a Q4-over-Q4 basis in 2026 before slowing to 2.6% in 2027.

Fiscal Policy
• Fiscal year (FY) 2026 ended on September 30, and the federal government tallied a $2.0 trillion
budget deficit (6.5% of GDP). We project the deficit to be unchanged in dollar terms in FY 2027

and a bit smaller as a share of GDP (~6%).
• Another continuing resolution to extend government funding past the December 11 deadline
seems like the most likely outcome at this time for the FY 2027 budget. It is possible Republicans
use the lame duck session of Congress to increase defense spending and raise the debt ceiling.
Looking ahead to the lame duck session of Congress that will follow the midterm elections, Congress
needs to pass a bill that extends government funding past December 11 to avert a government

shutdown. Another continuing resolution that punts the issue into Q1 seems like the most likely
outcome. Perhaps more interesting will be whether Republicans attempt to use budget reconciliation
to pass one more partisan piece of legislation. If they do, we suspect the two things at the top the
agenda will be more funding for national defense and immigration enforcement priorities as well as a
debt ceiling increase. For the latter, raising it in the lame duck would remove some leverage Democrats

could have in 2027 if they recapture one or both chambers. It is still very early, but we estimate the
debt ceiling "X date" by which Congress must act or face default is sometime in late Q3/early Q4 of
next year.
6 | Economics

### Page 7

Economic Outlook: October 2026 Economics
Monetary Policy & Interest Rates
• Our base case forecast is for the FOMC to hike the fed funds rate by 25 bps in December and
then keep the policy rate unchanged in 2027 at 4.00%-4.25%, with risks skewed toward additional
tightening.
As we go to print, the FOMC seems more likely than not to keep the fed funds rate unchanged in
October. But given the rationale under which the Fed started this tightening cycle and the forcefulness

with which Warsh has driven home his displeasure with elevated inflation, it seems entirely plausible
to us that October will be a live meeting. At a minimum, the recent inflation data seem to make a hike
in December a lock. Past December, the outlook will be highly dependent on future inflation data. Our
expectation is that core inflation will cool gradually in 2027, and we still do not see signs of demand-
side inflation in the data. But hiking cycles have an inertial component to them, and the recent inflation

data reset the clock to zero on the Fed seeing several good inflation prints in a row. This creates upside
risk to our base case for the fed funds rate.

International Developments
• Global growth remains resilient, leaving our 2026 and 2027 forecasts unchanged. Our inflation
forecasts are also unchanged, although risks of delayed price pass-through could prolong inflation
pressures into 2027.

• Monetary policy continues to lean toward tightening across advanced economies, while emerging
market central banks are increasingly cautious, either holding rates or easing more gradually as
inflation pressures rebuild.
The balance of risks to global inflation remains tilted to the upside, complicating the monetary policy
outlook even as growth has proven more resilient than expected. Elevated energy prices, particularly
for refined petroleum products, persistent geopolitical uncertainty and El Niño-related food price

risks could prolong inflationary pressures into 2027. While second-round effects still remain relatively
contained, repeated supply shocks are testing central banks’ willingness to look through the temporary
price increases, particularly where inflation expectations remain elevated, even as higher government
bond yields are already contributing to tighter financial conditions.
Across advanced economies, central banks face increasingly difficult trade-offs as persistent inflation

pressures limit the scope for policy accommodation. The Eurozone remains particularly vulnerable
to energy supply disruptions, with elevated natural gas prices and low storage levels adding to
inflation risks. We continue to expect a 25 bps European Central Bank hike in December, followed by a
prolonged hold through 2027. Fiscal concerns are also adding to pressure, particularly in France, where
the selloff in government debt has pushed yields higher. In the U.K., resilient activity and renewed

Economics | 7

### Page 8

Monthly Economics
inflation pressures similarly underpin our expectation for a Bank of England hike in November,
followed by a hold through 2027.
Elsewhere, policy paths are becoming more differentiated. After four hikes this year brought the Cash
Rate to 4.60%, we expect the Reserve Bank of Australia to remain on hold, although renewed inflation
pressures or stronger-than-expected activity could prompt further tightening. We also still expect
the Bank of Canada to hold at 2.25%, with subdued growth weighing against energy-driven inflation

risks, although incoming inflation data over the coming weeks will be critical in determining whether
persistent price pressures warrant tightening monetary policy perhaps as early as the December
meeting. Japan remains an exception to the broader hold outlook: while the yen's recovery provides
some relief from imported inflation, elevated energy prices and import dependence continue to pose
upside risks. We expect the Bank of Japan to hike again by year-end and now expect an additional 25

bps hike in Q3-2027, bringing the terminal rate to 1.75%.
The emerging market story has increasingly shifted from easing toward patience or selective
tightening. In Asia, China's weak domestic demand and persistent property-sector strains continue to
warrant policy support, with AI-related exports providing some offset. We expect a more gradual pace
of Reserve Requirement Ratio cuts alongside targeted monetary and fiscal measures. India faces the

opposite challenge, with elevated food and energy prices and rupee depreciation strengthening the
case for further Reserve Bank of India tightening. In Latin America, peso depreciation and elevated
energy costs pose upside inflation risks for Mexico, although limited exchange-rate pass-through
should allow Banxico to remain on hold while policymakers monitor underlying inflation and inflation
expectations. In Brazil, election-related uncertainty and renewed inflation pressures are likely to delay

further easing, with cuts expected to resume in Q1-2027 once policymakers see more convincing
evidence of disinflation.

## Document 302: Five ways the UK’s Autumn Budget could shock us

Source PDF: all report pdf/Five ways the UK’s Autumn Budget could shock us.pdf
Report source: ING Think
Date: 2026-10-16
Pages: 10
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

From better ﬁscal headroom to surprise income tax hikes, this
is how UK Chancellor John Healey's ﬁrst budget could surprise
investors
James Smith
UK Chancellor John Healey
In a nutshell: What we expect
Higher debt interest costs look set to drive the level of 'ﬁscal
headroom' from £24bn in March to £10bn. There's a growing
sense that Chancellor John Healey would like to get this up
to £15bn or higher. The government is set to modestly
increase spending on the cost of living, though the total

intervention is unlikely to meaningfully top £5bn and it's
likely to be mostly billed as temporary to avoid counting
against the main ﬁscal rule. We expect an increase in capital
spending of roughly £10bn/year, bearing in mind this is
exempt from the ﬁscal rule dictating that the current budget
must be in surplus after ﬁve years. To rebuild headroom

### Page 2

against that rule, we expect £5-6bn of tax hikes, likely via a
higher Bank Surcharge and possibly broadening the National
Insurance tax base.
In short, we don't expect the budget to deliver a material
stimulus (or add materially to the already-planned ﬁscal
consolidation). The impact on the Bank of England, which is
poised to hike rates in November, is likely to be fairly
minimal.
But all chancellors like to keep a rabbit in their hat – and what
follows are ﬁve ways it could surprise us.

1. Fiscal headroom goes up – not down
What if the UK’s ﬁscal hole isn’t nearly as bad as we think?
The widespread expectation going into the UK’s Autumn Budget on
28 October is that the Chancellor is going to have to face up to
higher debt interest costs with some modest tax hikes.
Bank of England rate expectations are almost a percentage point
higher than they were at the time of the Oﬃce for Budget
Responsibility’s March assessment of Britain’s public ﬁnances. 10-

year bond yields are some 70 basis points higher, which, taken
together, will push up debt interest spending by £11bn/year by
2029/30.
That points to a sizeable reduction in the amount of money the
government has left under its main ﬁscal rule, which requires the
current budget (day-to-day spending, including debt interest less
revenue) to be in surplus within three years. Back in March, that
surplus, or ﬁscal headroom, was projected at £24bn in 2029/30.

Debt interest costs are rising
Cookie settings

### Page 3

*Based on the average rate in the 10 days up to 25 September, which
likely represents the OBR's observation window for this budget
Source: Macrobond, ING
We think that headroom will have dropped to around £10bn before
any corrective action. And the vibe from the Treasury suggests the
Chancellor would prefer it to be above £15bn, to give more
breathing space in case of future adverse forecast revisions. That
would point to some limited tax hikes.
But what if that’s all wrong? The truth is, nobody really knows how

the OBR will change its assessment of the UK economy. And as we
saw last year, the Treasury is often inclined to talk up the doom and
gloom to open up a positive surprise on the day. 12 months ago,
the hit to headroom was considerably smaller than had been
widely expected.
This time, any positive surprise is most likely to come from wage
growth. Will the rise in energy prices spill into higher pay, just as it
did in 2022? We think it won’t, not least because the jobs market is

much weaker, an assessment we suspect the OBR will share.
But even relatively small upgrades to wage growth can make a big
diﬀerence to the government’s fortunes. Since 2010, the OBR has
revised the level of earnings across its ﬁve-year forecast by an
average of 1.3 percentage points in absolute terms. If it did that
now, say by lifting wage growth over the next two years by 0.6ppt
apiece, it would add £10bn to headroom, almost perfectly
oﬀsetting the hit from higher interest costs.

### Page 4

There are also reasons to think the impact of lower migration
projections, which some reports suggest could wipe another £7bn
oﬀ headroom via lower tax revenue forecasts, could be much less
than feared. The starting point for employment looks a little higher
than it did back in March. The rise in global equities this year should
also be helpful for capital gains tax projections.
Why does this all matter? Because the higher the headroom, the
less ‘need’ there is to hike taxes, and if it is signiﬁcantly higher than

expected, the more room there is to increase spending under the
ﬁscal rules.
The simple message is this: don’t judge the Autumn Budget by the
level of headroom left over.
2. Spending splurge
The problem with ‘headroom’ is not only that it’s highly sensitive to
the OBR’s economic outlook, but that the forward-looking nature of
the ﬁscal rules opens the door to a lot of creative accounting.
The impact of higher inﬂation is a case in point. This helps the

Treasury through higher receipts – both VAT and income tax. Yes, it
also pushes up the cost of indexed beneﬁts. But importantly,
departmental budgets don’t automatically increase to oﬀset the
impact of higher prices.
That opens up a classic trick from the Treasury, which is to leave
spending plans virtually unchanged in nominal terms, setting up
substantially smaller real-terms budget increases across the
medium term.
Even before the Iran War, government plans pencilled in real-terms

cuts to per capita departmental spending in FY2029. Given some
functions, like health, are ‘protected’, that implies much deeper cuts
to smaller departments.
Suﬃce it to say, that is unrealistic. Higher inﬂation will push up the
wage bill across government. It would cost £12bn to simply
maintain those March departmental spending plans in real terms.
That’s before considering the cost of ending that austerity pencilled

### Page 5

in for later this decade, or how the government will fund its defence
or social care ambitions.
A top-up to government departments is inevitable – the question is
when. It looks like the Treasury wants to kick the can on these tricky
budget decisions until next autumn, when it plans to release its
next Spending Review, a process of allocating money across
departments. No doubt the hope is that debt interest costs will
have fallen and the economic outlook brightened, such that there's

more space under the ﬁscal rules to make bigger spending
commitments.
That points to a very modest package of spending measures this
month, mainly targeting the cost of living. On top of the already-
announced cut to VAT on energy, we’d imagine the Treasury will
replicate last year’s budget and lower household bills by funding a
greater share of ‘policy costs’ from general taxation. This has the
added beneﬁt of lowering headline inﬂation at a particularly

sensitive moment for the Bank of England. Crucially though, the
government is likely to bill these moves as temporary, so they don’t
count under that main ﬁscal rule.
But if the economic picture really is better than feared, don’t rule
out the government going further. A decision to fully fund the UK’s
3% defence spending commitment would be a major wildcard,
though this would likely come at the expense of capital plans in
other areas of government.

Ultimately, the key thing to watch is the OBR’s assessment of how
all these policy changes aﬀect GDP growth and borrowing over the
next year. If there’s a material stimulus eﬀect, it could add to
market bets on UK rate hikes.
3. A manifesto break on income tax
Tax hikes are likely at the Autumn Budget, but should be very small.
We’re probably looking at a package of circa £5bn (0.2% of GDP).
Part of the problem is that the list of viable options looks perilously

small if Labour is to avoid breaking its manifesto pledge not to raise

the major taxes, which together account for around 70% of total

Our best guess is that the budget will include a hike in the Bank
Surcharge (raising £2bn if increased from 3% to 8%), together with
a broadening of the tax base for National Insurance to landlords
and partners in LLPs (together yielding around £2bn/year). Other
options, like ending a loophole on stamp duty for commercial
property or lowering the VAT threshold for businesses, have also

been on the table.
The problem with many of these measures is that they require
massive changes to stand any chance of raising material sums,
risking big behavioural reactions while oﬀering uncertain revenue
potential. There’s a reason why former chancellor Rachel Reeves
shied away from many of the measures we outline in the chart
below.

Is there scope for a surprise? We think there are two main ways on
tax – one good, one bad.
The good? A surprise announcement of a so-called ‘tax switch’, a
novel proposal from Resolution Foundation which would see the
government simultaneously raise basic income tax and cut

employee National Insurance by 2ppt. The impact on workers
would be neutral, but because income tax has a broader base
(including pensions), this would raise £6bn/year.
Though not a substantial sum, it would signal to investors that
Prime Minister Andy Burnham isn’t afraid to go near the thorny
issue of raising taxes. But in light of recent pension changes,

### Page 8

including to the infamous ‘triple lock’, this feels like too much of a
political gamble at this stage.
The negative surprise would be if the Treasury decided to raise the
basic tax allowance. Burnham ﬂirted with this on his ﬁrst day in
oﬃce; it would not only be expensive (potentially £10bn), but it
would also signal that the UK’s commitment to the threshold freeze
– which is doing the lion's share of the planned ﬁscal consolidation
– is waning.
A big rise in the National Living Wage

The minimum wage increase in April 2027, which will be
announced alongside the budget, is perhaps the most important
variable for the Bank of England.
This is based on a recommendation from the Low Pay Commission,
which earlier this year provisionally estimated the increase at 3.7%.
That’s a smaller increase than over the past two years and,
crucially, is not a million miles above the level the Bank considers
consistent with a 2% medium-term inﬂation target (3.25%).

But the LPC has some ﬂexibility in its recommendation, and one
criterion is the cost of living. Higher inﬂation means we could see a
ﬁnal proposal between 4% and 4.5%. Anything above that would,
we suspect, be problematic for the Bank of England and would
bolster the case for higher rates.
The government is no doubt well aware of this. And there appears
to be growing recognition among Labour ﬁgures, including on the
left of the party, that the big minimum wage increases, coupled

with previous payroll tax increases, have had a big impact on
business – particularly in consumer services.
But if the LPC were to recommend minimum wage growth of close
to 5%, could the government resist it? In theory, yes. But politically,
we suspect not.
A surge in bond issuance
We already know that bond issuance is set to increase again in
2027, though for reasons unrelated to the UK’s ﬁscal trajectory.

Still, the point is that, beneath these numbers, public sector net
borrowing is still likely to be projected to keep falling through the
rest of the decade. The major risk – related to some of the things

discussed earlier, like larger near-term spending or surprise
increases in tax allowances – would jeopardise this fundamental
improvement in bond issuance in the medium term. That's
something investors wouldn’t take kindly to.

The scenario, forecasts and nowcasts of the Economic Research

### Page 2

UNITED STATES 
The US economy is expected to grow above its potential in 2026, with an average annual growth rate of 2. 2%, close to the 2025 rate (2.1%) . 
This apparent resilience to the uncertainty, tariff and energy shocks, together with above-trend productivity growth, masks K-shaped growth, 
driven by investment linked to AI-optimism and consumption by the wealthiest amid a wealth effect (historically high stock market valuations). 
Inflation overshooting is set to continue (3.4% in 2026) at least through 2028, largely because of the rise in oil prices and tariffs – although the 
impact of these latter appears to be less significant than expected. The labour market is showing clear signs of improvement that should bring 
the unemployment rate down toward 4.0%. Given this shift in price and employment risks amid dynamic growth, the FOMC proceeded with a 
first rate hike at September’s meeting and we expect two further 25bp increases in December 2026 and January 2027, bringing the Fed Funds 
target range at 4,25% - 4,50%.

CHINA 
Economic growth slowed to 4.3% y/y in Q2 2026 vs. 5.0% in Q1. It is projected to reach 4.6% in 2026 as a whole, down from 5% in 2025. Growth 
remains characterized by a K-shaped trajectory, and the gap between the strong performance of exports and the fragility of sectors that rely 
on domestic demand  has even widened this year . Domestic demand has weakened significantly in recent months . Retail sales have barely 
increased, the crisis in the property sector has continued and household confidence remains low. Fiscal policy measures aimed at supporting 
economic growth are expected to increase in the second half of the year. CPI inflation has rebounded slightly since last March, mostly due to 
the rise in global energy prices, as well as due to the increase in electronic good prices and anti-involution measures implemented by the 
authorities. Deflationary pressures persist, however, given weakness in domestic demand.

EUROZONE 
Eurozone growth is expected to slow to 1.1% in 2026 (from 1.3% in 2025), before strengthening to 1.5% in 2027, supported by higher defence 
and infrastructure spending as well as the global AI capex cycle. The economy has proven resilient to the energy shock, notab ly thanks to the 
increasingly tangible impact of G erman defence spending on the real economy. Elevated energy prices in the autumn (especially for natural 
gas), combined with resilient growth, are set to accelerate inflation to a peak around the turn of the year. Annual average i nflation is thus 
expected to reach  3.0% in 2026 (from 2.1% in 2025), before easing to 2.8% in 2027. Given persistent inflation and resilient growth, we 
anticipate another 25bp hike from the ECB in December 2026, bringing the deposit rate to 2.75%, followed by an extended pause . That said, 
stronger than expected second-round effects could lead to more tightening than our current baseline.

### Page 3

FRANCE 
GDP growth remained low in Q2 (0% after -0.2% in Q1) as a result of deteriorated agricultural and construction output, as well as higher 
inflation (2.4% in 2026 according to our forecast, after 0.9% in 2025) . In 2026, GDP growth should  reach 0.5% (after 0.9% in 2025), driven 
mainly by exports.

UNITED KINGDOM 
UK GDP growth would ease slightly to 1.2% in 2026 (1.3% in 2025), before edging back to 1.3% in 2027. The economy has so far weathered 
both the energy price shock and domestic political volatility. That said, growth is expected to decelerate in the second half  of the year, while 
inflation is set to remain stubbornly above target (averaging 3.2% in both 2026 and 2027), driven by higher energy prices and mounting second-
round effects. In response, we expect the BoE to deliver a single insurance hike in November 2026, bringing the Bank Rate to 4.00%. The BoE 
is then expected to resume easing in H2 2027, with two cuts taking the Bank Rate to 3.50% by end-year.

JAPAN 
Following 1.2% in 2025, Japan's GDP growth is expected to decelerate to 1.0% in 2026, before rebounding to 1.2% in 2027. Higher inflation 
and production costs associated with the energy shock are expected to weigh on the economy’s overall performance. On the other hand, the 
latter is supported by fiscal policy  and AI-related investment. Inflation has generally overshot the 2% y/y target since 2022. Accordingly, the 
Bank of Japan initiated a cautious process of “adjustment in the degree of monetary accommodation” in 2024, lifting the policy rate to 1.25% 
so far (previously negative) – the highest since 1995. We expect two more hikes by the end of Q1 2027, followed by a return to a more cautious 
pace until the terminal rate of +2.5% is reached in H2 2028. Japan is facing long-term rates pressure, illustrated by historically high 10- and 30-
year yields, probably fueled by the level of public debt and the pace of monetary adjustment.

EXCHANGE RATES 
We expect Fed hikes to temporarily offset structural headwinds and support the dollar. EUR/USD would target 1.14 by end -2026, before the 
dollar's structural downtrend resumes toward 1.20 by end-2027, although political and fiscal risk may weigh on the euro next year. The USD/JPY 
appreciation would continue but at a more moderate pace, with the BoJ still behind the curve despite its hikes. By contrast, GBP/USD would 
see relative support underpinned by a modest fiscal risk premium under the new Labour government, and resilient growth.

The Goldman Sachs Group, Inc.
The US IPO market has reopened in a big way in 2026, with issuance already at a
record high. But this activity has raised two key concerns for the equity market:
whether it’s flashing a la te-cycle warning sign and whether the market can
comfortably digest so much new supply. So, how worried should investors be? The
IPO Initiative’s Jay Ritter, Acadian’s Owen Lamont, and GS’ Ben Snider agree that
we’re not in an “IPO wave” (yet) but differ on ho w much of a red flag the surge in

issuance is signaling. Snider argues that the late-cycle warning signs of past IPO
booms aren’t evident today and digestion concerns are overdone. Ritter notes that
while high new issuance volume has historically predicted lower future returns, the
signal has been weak. But Lamont is more cautious, calling an IPO wave one of the
“four horsemen” of a bubble (especially alongside a surge in debt issuance), but
stresses that IPO waves don’t necessarily signal the market top. Key to watch: firs t

day IPO returns and the all-important AI outlook.
Ben Snider, Chief US Equity Strategist, Goldman Sachs
“I’m less worried [about the surge in new issuance] than the average investor… the late-cycle warning signs that have
historically accompanied IPO booms aren’t evident today… [and] the equity supply-demand balance this year looks better
than most investors intuit.”
Jay Ritter, Director of The IPO Initiative and Professor Emeritus, The University of Florida’s Warrington College

of Business
“High new issue volume is a predictor of low future market returns. But, like most indicators, this signal works only slightly
better than chance… So, I wouldn’t view an IPO boom on its own as a sign that a market downturn is imminent.”
Owen Lamont, Senior Vice President and Portfolio Manager, Acadian Asset Management

### Page 3

El 
Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research 3 
Top of Mind Issue 150

After several relatively quiet years, the US IPO market has 
reopened in a big way in 2026, with issuance to date rising 
sharply to a new record high (and this isn’t just a US story—
see pg. 17). But this resurgence has raised two key concerns 
for the equity market: whether it’s flashing a late-cycle 
warning sign, and—even if it isn’t—whether the market can 
comfortably digest so much new issuance. So, how worried 
should investors be? 
We turn to several longtime market watchers for answers: 
Jay Ritter, Director of The IPO Initiative at the University of 
Florida’s Warrington College of Business, Owen Lamont, 
Senior Vice President and Portfolio Manager at Acadian Asset 
Management, and Ben Snider, GS Chief US Equity Strategist.  
We first ask whether the current moment constitutes an “IPO 
wave,” which has often preceded market downturns (see pg. 
8). All three agree that it doesn’t (at least not yet). Snider 
argues that while dollar volumes are already at record levels, 
the number of deals to date—which is much closer to the 
long-term average than past boom levels—suggests the 
current reopening is more of a normalization of IPO activity, 
amplified by a few large deals, than a true IPO boom. Ritter 
also points out that deal count remains fairly modest by 
historical standards owing to both cyclical and structural 
factors. And Lamont similarly notes that the market remains 
in an “IPO drought” by deal count, though he sees signs that 
an IPO and broader issuance wave may be forming. 
But with 2026 already a record issuance year, is this a red flag 
for the equity market? Snider says he is less concerned than 
the average investor, arguing that the late-cycle warning signs 
that have historically accompanied IPO booms aren’t evident 
today. In addition to the relatively unremarkable deal count, he 
notes that IPO valuations are only modestly above their long-
term average and well below prior boom levels. And Ritter 
adds that while high new issuance volume has been a past 
predictor of lower future market returns, the signal has been 
weak—working “only slightly better than chance.”  
But Lamont is more cautious. He acknowledges that higher 
issuance may simply reflect the capital demands of a 
transformative technology like AI. But he also points out that 
past bubbles have often been fueled by new technologies 
and have followed issuance and capex waves, as firms tend 
to sell equity when they believe it‘s overpriced. He therefore 
views an issuance wave as one of the “four horsemen” of a 
market bubble. That said, he stresses that IPO waves can 
last for years, so they “may mark the beginning of a bubble 
rather than the end.” And he also takes some comfort from 
the lack of extreme first day pops for recent IPOs, 
suggesting the absence of speculative euphoria. 
But perhaps the bigger concern is the market’s ability to 
digest the new issuance. Snider views such concerns as 
overdone given that the ~$700bn of corporate equity issuance 
he expects this year is modest relative to the size of the US 
equity market and the demand for US equities so far remains 
healthy. While he cautions that the supply-demand math will 
become more challenging in 2027 as investor lockups expire, 
he explains that some investors will choose to maintain their 
positions and those that don’t will likely buy other equities. He 
also notes a “self-limiting dynamic” to IPOs: they will come to 
market only as long as demand remains sufficient.   
Ritter also views digestion worries as overblown. He says 
that the $1.6tn of cash that US firms have annually returned 
to investors in recent years via dividends and buybacks must 
be recycled, so the IPO market—even with large deals—is 
absorbing just a fraction of the available capital. And Lamont 
notes that a threshold does exist at which increased supply 
will pressure prices. But he argues that where exactly it lies 
isn’t clear—for example, in the late 1990s, the market 
absorbed a large issuance wave for several years before 
prices began falling. (Note: the European equity market also 
faces indigestion concerns, but GS Global Equity Strategists 
Peter Oppenheimer and Guillaume Jaisson argue that 
Europe’s bigger challenge is a weak domestic equity bid, 
which they believe will constrain Europe’s IPO rebound).   
But adding to digestion concerns is the fact that rising 
issuance is not just an equity story. Firms are also turning to 
debt—primarily to fund the AI buildout—raising broader 
indigestion worries. Amanda Lynam, GS Chief Credit 
Strategist, argues that while the USD investment-grade 
market can absorb some of this debt issuance, issuer 
concentration and market saturation constraints will become 
more binding ahead, which she believes is underappreciated. 
Lamont, for his part, doesn’t view the AI-related debt boom 
as alarming on its own, but says the combination of a debt 
issuance wave and an equity issuance wave would be a 
negative sign for both credit and equity markets. 
Whether or not the rise in issuance is flashing warning signs 
for the broader market, investors must contend with the 
historical reality that IPOs tend to underperform in their first 
few years. So, how should investors navigate this? Lamont’s 
advice is simple: be patient. He says “IPOs are like bananas: 
they need to ripen before they’re ready to eat.” But Ritter 
cautions against generalizing the historical trend to the 
current IPO cohort. And Snider also argues for nuance, 
adding that investors can improve returns by owning a 
portfolio of IPOs rather than trying to pick a single winner. 
The performance question is even more important now that 
some major index providers have implemented new rules 
giving investors earlier access to large IPOs (see pg. 16). 
Ritter argues that earlier access makes sense for large new 
listings as long as it doesn’t create mechanical demand that 
distorts prices. But Lamont views the accelerated timeline 
more negatively, arguing that short seasoning periods could 
prevent proper price discovery.  
So, what should investors watch from here? Lamont is 
focused on whether the recent increase in activity becomes 
a wave and first day pops become more extreme. Snider is 
also watching first day returns as a key sentiment gauge as 
well as the all-important AI outlook—if it were to shift 
dramatically, he cautions that the outlook for both the 
market and associated IPOs would likely also shift.  
Allison Nathan, Editor  
Email: allison.nathan@gs.com     
Tel: 212-357-7504   
Goldman Sachs & Co. LLC

IPO surge: a red flag for markets?

### Page 4

El 
Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research 4 
Top of Mind Issue 150

Ben Snider is Chief US Equity Strategist at Goldman Sachs. Below, he argues that the current 
US IPO pickup reflects a return to normal rather than a true boom, with robust equity demand 
likely to absorb new supply and limit the risks to the broader market.  
Ashley Rhodes: How does this 
year’s reopening of the US IPO 
market compare to past IPO waves? 
Ben Snider: In dollar terms, 2026 is 
already a record year for the US IPO 
market. New issuance has totaled 
around $125bn year to date, 
surpassing the previous full-year 
record of around $120bn in 2021. We 
expect that momentum to continue, with total US IPO volume 
likely to exceed $200bn this year. 
However, in terms of the number of IPOs, the picture looks far 
less extreme. Roughly 60 US companies have gone public so 
far this year. While that represents a greater than 50% 
increase yoy and marks the strongest start to a year since 
2021, it is much closer to the long-term average than to past 
boom levels. The 25-year median of IPOs per year is around 
100, and the market is roughly on track for that in 2026. By 
comparison, over 250 companies went public in 2021, and 
nearly 400 did so in 1999 at the height of the Dot-Com Boom. 
So, while dollar volumes are at record levels, the current 
reopening looks more like a normalization of IPO activity—
amplified by a few very large deals—than the broad-based 
surge investors typically associate with an IPO boom. 
Ashley Rhodes: Does the concentration of large deals 
suggest that only the biggest, most mature companies can 
come to market? 
Ben Snider: No. The IPO market has reopened broadly, with 
deal activity happening across sectors—obviously including 
tech but also industrials and healthcare—as well as among 
smaller, earlier stage companies. But driving the outsized dollar 
volume to date is a handful of very large technology 
companies. So, the concentration of IPO dollars reflects a few 
very large deals and the strength of the tech/AI theme more 
than a market that’s only open to the largest firms. 
Ashley Rhodes: Why now for a pickup in IPO activity when 
the last several years have been relatively quiet? 
Ben Snider: More than any other factor, the macro backdrop 
has become more supportive after several challenging years. In 
2022, the sharpest Fed hiking cycle in history drove a sharp 
decline in valuations—particularly for the long-duration, high-
growth companies that typically represent the majority of the 
IPO pipeline—making it very difficult for many private firms to 
go public. Since then, interest rates have stopped rising, equity 
valuations have recovered, and both the economy and 
corporate profits have continued to grow. So, companies are 
getting back to the high-water mark of a few years ago in 
terms of total value, making going public more viable. Our IPO 
Barometer—which measures how conducive the macro 
environment is to new issuance—reflects this improvement. 
But, again, it’s indicating a more normal environment for IPO 
activity rather than boom conditions. 
Even so, the more supportive backdrop has also given private 
equity (PE) investors the opportunity to take a backlog of 
mature portfolio companies public. The motivation to do so has 
been in place for years given muted returns of capital and a 
growing desire for liquidity, but market conditions and 
valuations have been unfavorable. With a record $4tn in 
unrealized PE portfolio value and the IPO market now open for 
business, those investors are increasingly taking advantage. 
There is also a micro overlay to the pickup, tied to AI. The AI 
boom is, in many ways, a capex boom and the enormous 
funding required to build AI infrastructure has driven unusually 
large deal sizes among a relatively small number of companies. 
This dynamic is also driving a large increase in equity and debt 
issuance among established public companies. 
Ashley Rhodes: Would we be talking about an IPO boom 
at all if the AI phenomenon hadn’t taken hold? 
Ben Snider: Given the more supportive macro and market 
backdrop, we would likely still be experiencing the strongest 
IPO activity since 2021. But without the AI theme driving a 
handful of very large deals to date, dollar volumes would be 
much lower, and most investors would likely be characterizing 
the environment as a return to normal rather than as a boom. 
Ashley Rhodes: Despite that, many investors seem to be 
viewing the pickup in IPO activity as a warning sign for the 
equity market. What are investors’ main concerns? 
Ben Snider: A tremendous amount of investor concern exists, 
some of it stemming from the inclusion of IPO booms on the 
checklist of warning signs that typically come late in the cycle. 
In 1999 and 2021, elevated IPO activity coincided with 
stretched valuations and exuberant investor sentiment, and 
market downturns followed. More broadly, periods of heavy 
IPO activity have often preceded below-average equity market 
returns. So, investors are understandably wondering whether 
the current pickup is a sign of market froth or a potential peak. 
But the bigger and more universal investor concern revolves 
around the supply-demand balance in the equity market. The 
fear is that the substantial increase in equity supply will 
overwhelm the market and ultimately lead to lower prices. 
Ashley Rhodes: Are those concerns warranted? 
Ben Snider: I’m less worried than the average investor for a 
few reasons. First, the late-cycle warning signs that have 
historically accompanied IPO booms aren’t evident today. As 
we’ve discussed, IPO activity is rising, but it’s not extreme. 
And despite the relatively common narrative of stretched 
equity valuations, IPO valuations are only modestly above their 
long-term average and well below the elevated levels of prior 
IPO booms. To put some numbers on this, the median IPO last 
Interview with Ben Snider

### Page 5

El 
Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research 5 
Top of Mind Issue 150

year traded at 5x EV/sales versus the 30-year median of 
roughly 4x and compared to 9x in 1999 and 7x in 2021.  
Second, while the dollar amount of incremental supply is large, 
it’s modest relative to the overall size of the equity market. Our 
estimate for roughly $700bn of US corporate equity issuance in 
2026—including IPOs, follow-ons, converts, and SPACs—
equates to only around 1% of Russell 3000 market 
capitalization. This is in line with the 2015-2019 average and 
below the roughly 1.5% in 2021 and 2% at the height of the 
Dot-Com Boom.  
Third, data to date suggests that broader demand for US 
equities remains healthy. On the corporate side, S&P 500 
buybacks are up 4% yoy in 1Q26—continuing the recent trend 
of mid-single digit growth—and strategic M&A announcements 
have risen 100% yoy. On the household side, households 
were net sellers of equities during the Dot-Com Boom, but 
have shifted to net buyers in recent years via ETF and mutual 
fund inflows as well as robust retail trading activity. The share 
of the US equity market owned by foreign investors has also 
risen from 6% in 1995 to 18% today, with foreign investors 
continuing to add exposure despite last year’s pullback 
concerns. All told, the equity supply-demand balance this year 
looks better than most investors intuit. 
Ashley Rhodes: But aren’t corporate buybacks slowing? 
Ben Snider: A common misconception is that the AI capex 
buildout has essentially halted buybacks. It’s true that 
hyperscalers have pulled back on repurchases to fund their AI 
efforts, and buyback growth has slowed as a result. Scaling 
buybacks to market cap, the buyback yield is on track to be the 
lowest in around 15-20 years outside of recessions. But it’s still 
solidly positive, and, importantly, around 100bp higher than in 
the late 1990s. So, aggregate corporate demand remains 
healthy, with many companies outside of the hyperscalers—
particularly Banks and AI capex beneficiaries such as 
semiconductors—still expanding repurchase programs. Nvidia, 
for example, recently added $80bn to its buyback authorization, 
and total US buyback announcements are running at a record 
$960bn year to date. Looking ahead, we expect roughly $1.3tn 
in gross corporate buybacks in 2026, which should more than 
offset the incremental equity supply from both direct corporate 
issuance and potential post-lockup supply this year.  
Ashley Rhodes: How will the supply-demand picture shift 
ahead? 
Ben Snider: The supply-demand balance is clearly moving in 
the wrong direction, and the math admittedly becomes more 
challenging in 2027 as the lockups from this year’s IPOs 
expire. When a company goes public, typically only a portion of 
its shares becomes immediately available to trade. The rest 
remains in private hands, and those investors are restricted 
from selling in the public market for a designated period, 
traditionally for around six months after listing. Once that 
lockup period expires, those investors can sell, and the 
company’s public float—or the shares available for sale—can 
increase quite dramatically. That consideration may help 
explain why recent IPOs have struggled more than usual in 
their first few months of trading as some investors appear 
reluctant to buy before the final supply impact becomes clear.  
That said, concerns about the potential supply headwind are 
likely overdone. One reason is that unlocked shares don’t 
automatically translate into new public supply. Many investors 
will choose to maintain their ownership. And even when they 
do sell, some of the proceeds will likely be recycled back into 
other equities, creating at least a partial offset to new supply.  
Importantly, equity issuance also has a self-limiting dynamic. 
IPOs will continue to come to market only as long as demand 
remains sufficient, which requires equity prices and valuations 
to remain reasonably robust. If the market starts to struggle 
with too much supply, that will naturally limit future issuance 
and become self-correcting.  
Ashley Rhodes: Historically, IPOs tend to underperform the 
broader market for a few years after listing. Do you expect 
a similar pattern, and what are the investor implications?  
Ben Snider: While the median IPO has historically 
underperformed the broader market, the average IPO has 
outperformed the median, reflecting a right tail of firms that 
have delivered exceptionally strong returns. This is also why an 
equal-weighted portfolio of IPOs has generally outperformed 
the broader market over time—and why, if history repeats as I 
expect, investors are likely better served owning a portfolio of 
IPOs rather than trying to pick a single winner. Within that 
framework, investors should focus on the fundamental 
variables that have historically been linked to stronger post-IPO 
performance: valuation, revenue growth, and profitability. 
Firms that go public with lower multiples, higher revenue 
growth, and a shorter path to profitability typically post better 
returns. 
Ashley Rhodes: What risks could derail the IPO market’s 
current momentum? 
Ben Snider: Beyond the obvious macro risk of an economic 
slowdown, the more specific micro risk is the one we’ve been 
discussing: that the supply of new equity exceeds the market’s 
willingness to absorb it. A material deterioration in early post-
IPO performance would indicate that the market is struggling 
with the supply-demand balance. In that sense, first day 
returns will be important to watch—IPOs that fail to deliver 
initial first day gains may signal weaker demand and a less 
favorable outlook for new issuance. Likewise, consistently 
poor returns in the early weeks of trading would likely 
disincentivize investors and corporate managements from 
participating in future deals and serve as a self-regulating 
constraint on further issuance.  
It’s also important to acknowledge the role of AI. Investor 
appetite for AI-exposed stocks and surging AI capex spending 
have been major tailwinds for the broad equity market and are 
key reasons to expect more large private companies to go 
public. If the AI outlook were to change dramatically—whether 
due to earnings disappointments, a shift in capex expectations, 
or a broader reassessment of valuations—the outlook for both 
the market and associated IPOs would likely shift as well.
